We slept in what had once been the gymnasium. The floor was of varnished woo
ith stripes and circles painted on it, for the games that were formerly played there; th
oops for the basketball nets were still in place, though the nets were gone. A balcon
an around the room, for the spectators, and I thought I could smell, faintly like a
fterimage, the pungent scent of sweat, shot through with the sweet taint of chewin
um and perfume from the watching girls, felt-skirted as I knew from pictures, later
ini-skirts, then pants, then in one earring, spiky green-streaked hair. Dances wou
ave been held there; the music lingered, a palimpsest of unheard sound, style upo
yle, an undercurrent of drums, a forlorn wail, garlands made of tissue-paper flower
ardboard devils, a revolving ball of mirrors, powdering the dancers with a snow
There was old sex in the room and loneliness, and expectation, of something withou
shape or name. I remember that yearning, for something that was always about t
appen and was never the same as the hands that were on us there and then, in th
mall of the back, or out back, in the parking lot, or in the television room with th
ound turned down and only the pictures flickering over lifting flesh.
We yearned for the future. How did we learn it, that talent for insatiability? It was
he air; and it was still in the air, an afterthought, as we tried to sleep, in the army co
hat had been set up in rows, with spaces between so we could not talk. We ha
annelette sheets, like children's, and army-issue blankets, old ones that still said u.
We folded our clothes neatly and laid them on the stools at the ends of the beds. Th
ghts were turned down but not out. Aunt Sara and Aunt Elizabeth patrolled; they ha
ectric cattle prods slung on thongs from their leather belts.
No guns though, even they could not be trusted with guns. Guns were for the guard
pecially picked from the Angels. The guards weren't allowed inside the building excep
hen called, and we weren't allowed out, except for our walks, twice daily, two by tw
round the football field which was enclosed now by a chain-link fence topped wit
arbed wire. The Angels stood outside it with their backs to us. They were objects of fea
us, but of something else as well. If only they would look. If only we could talk t
hem. Something could be exchanged, we thought, some deal made, some trade-off, w
ill had our bodies. That was our fantasy.
We learned to whisper almost without sound. In the semi-darkness we could stretc
ut our arms, when the Aunts weren't looking, and touch each other's hands acro
pace. We learned to lip-read, our heads flat on the beds, turned sideways, watchin
ach other's mouths. In this way we exchanged names, from bed to bed:
Alma. Janine. Dolores. Moira. June.

A chair, a table, a lamp. Above, on the white ceiling, a relief ornament in the shape
wreath, and in the centre of it a blank space, plastered over, like the place in a fac
here the eye has been taken out. There must have been a chandelier, once. They'v
moved anything you could tie a rope to.
A window, two white curtains. Under the window, a window seat with a litt
ushion. When the window is partly open – it only opens partly – the air can come
nd make the curtains move. I can sit in the chair, or on the window seat, hands folde
nd watch this. Sunlight comes in through the window too, and falls on the floor, whic
made of wood, in narrow strips, highly polished. I can smell the polish. There's a ru
n the floor, oval, of braided rags. This is the kind of touch they like: folk art, archai
ade by women, in their spare time, from things that have no further use. A return t
aditional values. Waste not want not. I am not being wasted. Why do I want?
On the wall above the chair, a picture, framed but with no glass: a print of flower
ue irises, watercolour. Flowers are still allowed. Does each of us have the same prin
he same chair, the same white curtains, I wonder? Government issue?
Think of it as being in the army, said Aunt Lydia.
A bed. Single, mattress medium-hard, covered with a flocked white spread. Nothin
kes place in the bed but sleep; or no sleep. I try not to think too much. Like oth
hings now, thought must be rationed. There's a lot that doesn't bear thinking abou
hinking can hurt your chances, and I intend to last. I know why there is no glass,
ont of the watercolour picture of blue irises, and why the window only opens part
nd why the glass in it is shatterproof. It isn't running away they're afraid of. W
ouldn't get far. It's those other escapes, the ones you can open in yourself, given
utting edge.
So. Apart from these details, this could be a college guest room, for the le
stinguished visitors; or a room in a rooming house, of former times, for ladies
duced circumstances. That is what we are now. The circumstances have been reduce
r those of us who still have circumstances.
But a chair, sunlight, flowers: these are not to be dismissed. I am alive, I live,
reathe, I put my hand out, unfolded, into the sunlight. Where I am is not a prison but
rivilege, as Aunt Lydia said, who was in love with either/or.
he bell that measures time is ringing. Time here is measured by bells, as once
unneries. As in a nunnery too, there are few mirrors.
I get up out of the chair, advance my feet into the sunlight, in their red shoes, fla

eeled to save the spine and not for dancing. The red gloves are lying on the bed. I pic
hem up, pull them onto my hands, finger by finger. Everything except the wing

ull, gathered to a flat yoke that extends over the breasts, the sleeves are full. The whi
ings too are prescribed issue; they are to keep us from seeing, but also from bein
en. I never looked good in red, it's not my colour. I pick up the shopping basket, put

The door of the room – not my room, I refuse to say my – is not locked. In fact
oesn't shut properly. I go out into the polished hallway, which has a runner down th
entre, dusty pink. Like a path through the forest, like a carpet for royalty, it shows m

The carpet bends and goes down the front staircase and I go with it, one hand on th
anister, once a tree, turned in another century, rubbed to a warm gloss. Late Victorian
he house is, a family house, built for a large rich family. There's a grandfather clock

There remains a mirror, on the hall wall. If I turn my head so that the white wing

onvex, a pier-glass, like the eye of a fish, and myself in it like a distorted shadow,

oment of carelessness that is the same as danger. A Sister, dipped in blood.
At the bottom of the stairs there's a hat-and-umbrella stand, the bentwood kind, lon
ounded rungs of wood curving gently up into hooks shaped like the opening fronds of
rn. There are several umbrellas in it: black, for the Commander, blue, for th
ommander's Wife, and the one assigned to me, which is red. I leave the red umbrel
here it is, because I know from the window that the day is sunny. I wonder whether o
ot the Commander's Wife is in the sitting room. She doesn't always sit. Sometimes I ca
ear her pacing back and forth, a heavy step and then a light one, and the soft tap
er cane on the dusty-rose carpet.
walk along the hallway, past the sitting-room door and the door that leads into th
ning room, and open the door at the end of the hall and go through into the kitchen
ere the smell is no longer of furniture polish. Rita is in here, standing at the kitche
ble, which has a top of chipped white enamel. She's in her usual Martha's dress, whic
dull green, like a surgeon's gown of the time before. The dress is much like mine
hape, long and concealing, but with a bib apron over it and without the white wing
nd the veil. She puts the veil on to go outside, but nobody much cares who sees the fac
f a Martha. Her sleeves are rolled to the elbow, showing her brown arms. She's makin

Rita sees me and nods, whether in greeting or in simple acknowledgement of m
resence it's hard to say, and wipes her floury hands on her apron and rummages in th
tchen drawer for the token book. Frowning, she tears out three tokens and hands the
me. Her face might be kindly if she would smile. But the frown isn't personal: it's th
d dress she disapproves of, and what it stands for. She thinks I may be catching, like
sease or any form of bad luck.
Sometimes I listen outside closed doors, a thing I never would have done in the tim
efore. I don't listen long, because I don't want to be caught doing it. Once, though,
eard Rita say to Cora that she wouldn't debase herself like that.
Nobody asking you, Cora said. Anyways, what could you do, supposing?
Go to the Colonies, Rita said. They have the choice.
With the Unwomen, and starve to death and Lord knows what all? said Cora. Catc
They were shelling peas; even through the almost-closed door I could hear the ligh
ink of the hard peas falling into the metal bowl. I heard Rita, a grunt or a sigh,
rotest or agreement.
Anyways, they're doing it for us all, said Cora, or so they say. If I hadn't of got m
ubes tied, it could of been me, say I was ten years younger. It's not that bad. It's n
hat you'd call hard work.
Better her than me, Rita said, and I opened the door. Their faces were the wa
omen's faces are when they've been talking about you behind your back and the
hink you've heard: embarrassed, but also a little defiant, as if it were their right. Th
ay, Cora was more pleasant to me than usual, Rita more surly.
Today, despite Rita's closed face and pressed lips, I would like to stay here, in th
tchen. Cora might come in, from somewhere else in the house, carrying her bottle
mon oil and her duster, and Rita would make coffee – in the houses of th
ommanders there is still real coffee – and we would sit at Rita's kitchen table, which
ot Rita's any more than my table is mine, and we would talk, about aches and pain
lnesses, our feet, our backs, all the different kinds of mischief that our bodies, lik
nruly children, can get up to. We would nod our heads as punctuation to each other
oices, signalling that yes, we know all about it. We would exchange remedies and tr
outdo each other in the recital of our physical miseries; gently we would complain
ur voices soft and minor-key and mournful as pigeons in the eaves troughs. I know wh
ou mean, we'd say. Or, a quaint expression you sometimes hear, still, from old
eople: I hear where you're coming from, as if the voice itself were a traveller, arrivin
om a distant place. Which it would be, which it is.
How I used to despise such talk. Now I long for it. At least it was talk. An exchange,
orts.
Or we would gossip. The Marthas know things, they talk among themselves, passin
he unofficial news from house to house. Like me, they listen at doors, no doubt, and se

Fraternize means to behave like a brother. Luke told me that. He said there was n

"All right," I say. I don't smile. Why tempt her to friendship?

go out by the back door, into the garden, which is large and tidy: a lawn in th

This garden is the domain of the Commander's Wife. Looking out through m

I once had a garden. I can remember the smell of the turned earth, the plump shap

ould pass more swiftly that way. Sometimes the Commander's Wife has a chair brough

Sometimes I think these scarves aren't sent to the Angels at all, but unravelled an

What does she envy me?
She doesn't speak to me, unless she can't avoid it. I am a reproach to her; and

As for my husband, she said, he's just that. My husband. I want that to be perfect

Yes, Ma'am, I said again, forgetting. They used to have dolls, for little girls, th
ould talk if you pulled a string at the back; I thought I was sounding like that, voice

It's one of the things we fought for, said the Commander's Wife, and suddenly sh

The first time was on television, when I was eight or nine. It was when my moth
as sleeping in, on Sunday mornings, and I would get up early and go to the televisio

The woman sitting in front of me was Serena Joy. Or had been, once. So it was wor
han I thought.

I remind you that this was not the first such discovery. You are doubtless familiar, fo
stance, with the item known as "The A.B. Memoirs," located in a garage in a suburb
eattle, and with "The Diary of P.," excavated by accident during the erection of a ne
eeting house in the vicinity of what was once Syracuse, New York.
Professor Wade and I were very excited by this new discovery. Luckily we had, sever
ears before, with the aid of our excellent resident antiquarian technician, reconstructe
machine capable of playing such tapes, and we immediately set about the painstakin
ork of transcription.
There were some thirty tapes in the collection altogether, with varying proportions
usic to spoken word. In general, each tape begins with two or three songs, a
amouflage no doubt: then the music is broken off and the speaking voice takes ove
he voice is a woman's and, according to our voice-print experts, the same on
hroughout. The labels on the cassettes were authentic period labels, dating, of cours

as banned under the regime. There were, for instance, four tapes entitled "Elv
resley's Golden Years," three of "Folk Songs of Lithuania," three of "Boy George Tak
Off," and two of "Mantovani's Mellow Strings," as well as some titles that sported
ere single tape each: "Twisted Sister at Carnegie Hall" is one of which I a
articularly fond.
Although the labels were authentic, they were not always appended to the tape wit
he corresponding songs. In addition, the tapes were arranged in no particular orde
eing loose at the bottom of the box; nor were they numbered. Thus it was up t
rofessor Wade and myself to arrange the blocks of speech in the order in which the
ppeared to go; but, as I have said elsewhere, all such arrangements are based on som
uesswork and are to be regarded as approximate, pending further research.
Once we had the transcription in hand – and we had to go over it several time
wing to the difficulties posed by accent, obscure referents, and archaisms – we had t
ake some decision as to the nature of the material we had thus so laboriously acquire
everal possibilities confronted us. First, the tapes might be a forgery. As you know
here have been several instances of such forgeries, for which publishers have paid larg
ums, wishing to trade no doubt on the sensationalism of such stories. It appears th
ertain periods of history quickly become, both for other societies and for those th
llow them, the stuff of not especially edifying legend and the occasion for a good de
f hypocritical self-congratulation. If I may be permitted an editorial aside, allow me t
ay that in my opinion we must be cautious about passing moral judgement upon th
ileadeans. Surely we have learned by now that such judgements are of necessit
ulture-specific. Also, Gileadean society was under a good deal of pressure, demograph
nd otherwise, and was subject to factors from which we ourselves are happily mor
ee. Our job is not to censure but to understand. (Applause.)
To return from my digression: tape like this, however, is very difficult to fak
onvincingly, and we were assured by the experts who examined them that the physic

bjects themselves are genuine. Certainly the recording itself, that is, th

Supposing, then, the tapes to be genuine, what of the nature of the account itsel

as a whiff of emotion recollected, if not in tranquillity, at least post facto.
If we could establish an identity for the narrator, we felt, we might be well on th
ay to an explanation of how this document – let me call it that for the sake of brevit
came into being. To do this, we tried two lines of investigation.
First, we attempted, through old town plans of Bangor and other remainin
ocumentation, to identify the inhabitants of the house that must have occupied the si

afe house" on the Underground Femaleroad during our period, and our author ma

Unfortunately, this trail led nowhere. Possibly these people, if they had indeed been
nk in the underground chain, had been discovered and arrested, in which case an
ocumentation referring to them would have been destroyed. So we pursued a secon
ne of attack. We searched records of the period, trying to correlate known historic
ersonages with the individuals who appear in our author's account. The survivin
cords of the time are spotty, as the Gileadean regime was in the habit of wiping i
wn computers and destroying printouts after various purges and internal upheaval
ut some printouts remain. Some indeed were smuggled to England, for propaganda u

t that time.
We held out no hope of tracing the narrator herself directly. It was clear from intern
vidence that she was among the first wave of women recruited for reproductiv
urposes and allotted to those who both required such services and could lay claim t
hem through their position in the elite. The regime created an instant pool of suc
omen by the simple tactic of declaring all second marriages and non-marital liaison
dulterous, arresting the female partners, and, on the grounds that they were moral
nfit, confiscating the children they already had, who were adopted by childless coupl
f the upper echelons who were eager for progeny by any means. (In the middle perio

--- PAGE 230 ---
his policy was extended to cover all marriages not contracted within the state church
en highly placed in the regime were thus able to pick and choose among women wh
ad demonstrated their reproductive fitness by having produced one or more health
hildren, a desirable characteristic in an age of plummeting Caucasian birth rates,
henomenon observable not only in Gilead but in most northern Caucasian societies
he time.
The reasons for this decline are not altogether clear to us. Some of the failure t
produce can undoubtedly be traced to the widespread availability of birth control
arious kinds, including abortion, in the immediate pre-Gilead period. Some infertilit
hen, was willed, which may account for the differing statistics among Caucasians an
on-Caucasians; but the rest was not. Need I remind you that this was the age of the R
rain syphilis and also the infamous AIDS epidemic, which, once they spread to th
opulation at large, eliminated many young sexually active people from th
productive pool? Stillbirths, miscarriages, and genetic deformities were widesprea
nd on the increase, and this trend has been linked to the various nuclear-plan
ccidents, shutdowns, and incidents of sabotage that characterized the period, as well a
leakages from chemical and biological-warfare stockpiles and toxic-waste dispos
tes, of which there were many thousands, both legal and illegal – in some instanc
hese materials were simply dumped into the sewage system – and to the uncontrolle
se of chemical insecticides, herbicides, and other sprays.
But whatever the causes, the effects were noticeable, and the Gilead regime was n
he only one to react to them at the time. Romania, for instance, had anticipated Gilea
the eighties by banning all forms of birth control, imposing compulsory pregnanc
sts on the female population, and linking promotion and wage increases to fertility.
The need for what I may call birth services was already recognized in the pre-Gilea
eriod, where it was being inadequately met by "artificial insemination," "fertilit
inics," and the use of "surrogate mothers," who were hired for the purpose. Gilea
utlawed the first two as irreligious, but legitimized and enforced the third, which wa
onsidered to have biblical precedents; they thus replaced the serial polygamy commo
the pre-Gilead period with the older form of simultaneous polygamy practised both
arly Old Testament times and in the former State of Utah in the nineteenth century. A
e know from the study of history, no new system can impose itself upon a previou
ne without incorporating many of the elements to be found in the latter, as witness th
agan elements in mediaeval Christianity and the evolution of the Russian "K.G.B." fro
he Czarist secret service that preceded it; and Gilead was no exception to this rule. I
acist policies, for instance, were firmly rooted in the pre-Gilead period, and racist fea
rovided some of the emotional fuel that allowed the Gilead takeover to succeed as we
s it did.
Our author, then, was one of many, and must be seen within the broad outlines of th
oment in history of which she was a part. But what else do we know about her, apa
om her age, some physical characteristics that could be anyone's, and her place

sidence? Not very much. She appears to have been an educated woman, insofar as
raduate of any North American college of the time may be said to have been educate
Laughter, some groans.) But the woods, as you say, were full of these, so that is no help
he does not see fit to supply us with her original name, and indeed all official record
f it would have been destroyed upon her entry into the Rachel and Leah Re-educatio
entre. "Offred" gives no clue, since, like "Ofglen" and "Ofwarren," it was
atronymic, composed of the possessive preposition and the first name of the gentlema
question. Such names were taken by these women upon their entry into a connectio
ith the household of a specific Commander, and relinquished by them upon leaving it
The other names in the document are equally useless for the purposes of identificatio
nd authentication. "Luke" and "Nick" drew blanks, as did "Moira" and "Janine." Ther
a high probability that these were, in any case, pseudonyms, adopted to protect the
dividuals should the tapes be discovered. If so, this would substantiate our view th
he tapes were made inside the borders of Gilead, rather than outside, to be smuggle
ack for use by the Mayday underground.
Elimination of the above possibilities left us with one remaining. If we could identif
he elusive "Commander," we felt, at least some progress would have been made. W
rgued that such a highly placed individual had probably been a participant in the fir
f the top-secret Sons of Jacob Think Tanks, at which the philosophy and social structur
f Gilead were hammered out. These were organized shortly after the recognition of th
uperpower arms stalemate and the signing of the classified Spheres of Influence Accor
hich left the superpowers free to deal, unhampered by interference, with the growin
umber of rebellions within their own empires. The official records of the Sons of Jaco
eetings were destroyed after the middle-period Great Purge, which discredited an
quidated a number of the original architects of Gilead; but we have access to som
formation through the diary kept in cipher by Wilfred Limpkin, one of th
ociobiologists present. (As we know, the socio-biological theory of natural polygam
as used as a scientific justification for some of the odder practices of the regime, just a
arwinism was used by earlier ideologies.)
From the Limpkin material we know that there are two possible candidates, that i
wo whose names incorporate the element "Fred": Frederick R. Waterford and B
rederick Judd. No photographs survive of either, although Limpkin describes the latt
s a stuffed shirt, and, I quote, "somebody for whom foreplay is what you do on a go
ourse." (Laughter.) Limpkin himself did not long survive the inception of Gilead, and w
ave his diary only because he foresaw his own end and placed it with his sister-in-la
Calgary.
Waterford and Judd both have characteristics that recommend them to us. Waterfor
ossessed a background in market research, and was, according to Limpkin, responsib
r the design of the female costumes and for the suggestion that the Handmaids wea
d, which he seems to have borrowed from the uniforms of German prisoners of war
anadian "P.O.W." camps of the Second World War era. He seems to have been th
riginator of the term "Particicution " which he lifted from an exercise program popula

--- PAGE 232 ---
ometime in the last third of the century; the collective rope ceremony, however, wa
uggested by an English village custom of the seventeenth century. "Salvaging" ma
ave been his too, although by the time of Gilead's inception it had spread from i
rigin in the Philippines to become a general term for the elimination of one's politic
nemies. As I have said elsewhere, there was little that was truly original with o
digenous to Gilead: its genius was synthesis.
Judd, on the other hand, seems to have been less interested in packaging and mor
oncerned with tactics. It was he who suggested the use of an obscure "C.I.A." pamphl
n the destabilization of foreign governments as a strategic handbook for the Sons
acob, and he, too, who drew up the early hit-lists of prominent "Americans" of th

ust have required maximum infiltration of the security system surrounding Congres
nd without which the Constitution could never have been suspended. The Nation
omelands and the Jewish boat-person plan were both his, as was the idea
rivatizing the Jewish repatriation scheme, with the result that more than one boatloa
f Jews was simply dumped into the Atlantic, to maximize profits. From what we kno
f Judd, this would not have bothered him much. He was a hard-liner, and is credited b
mpkin with the remark, "Our big mistake was teaching them to read. We won't d
hat again."
It is Judd who is credited with devising the form, as opposed to the name, of th
articicution ceremony, arguing that it was not only a particularly horrifying an
ffective way of ridding yourself of subversive elements, but that it would also act as
eam valve for the female elements in Gilead. Scapegoats have been notoriously usef
hroughout history, and it must have been most gratifying for these Handmaids, s
gidly controlled at other times, to be able to tear a man apart with their bare hand
very once in a while. So popular and effective did this practice become that it wa
gularized in the middle period, when it took place four times a year, on solstices an
quinoxes. There are echoes here of the fertility rites of early Earth-goddess cults. As w
eard at the panel discussion yesterday afternoon, Gilead was, although undoubted
atriarchal in form, occasionally matriarchal in content, like some sectors of the soci
bric that gave rise to it. As the architects of Gilead knew, to institute an effectiv
talitarian system or indeed any system at all you must offer some benefits an
eedoms, at least to a privileged few, in return for those you remove.
In this connection a few comments upon the crack female control agency known a
he "Aunts" is perhaps in order. Judd – according to the Limpkin material – was of th
pinion from the outset that the best and most cost-effective way to control women fo
productive and other purposes was through women themselves. For this there wer
any historical precedents; in fact, no empire imposed by force or otherwise has ev
een without this feature: control of the indigenous by members of their own group. I
he case of Gilead, there were many women willing to serve as Aunts, either because
genuine belief in what they called "traditional values," or for the benefits they migh
hereby acquire. When power is scarce, a little of it is tempting. There was, too,

--- PAGE 233 ---
egative inducement: childless or infertile or older women who were not married cou
ke service in the Aunts and thereby escape redundancy, and consequent shipment t
he infamous Colonies, which were composed of portable populations used mainly a
xpendable toxic cleanup squads, though if lucky you could be assigned to le
azardous tasks, such as cotton picking and fruit harvesting.
The idea, then, was Judd's, but the implementation has the mark of Waterford upo
Who else among the Sons of Jacob Think-Tankers would have come up with th
otion that the Aunts should take names derived from commercial products available t
omen in the immediate pre-Gilead period, and thus familiar and reassuring to them
he names of cosmetic lines, cake mixes, frozen desserts, and even medicinal remedie
was a brilliant stroke, and confirms us in our opinion that Waterford was, in h
rime, a man of considerable ingenuity. So, in his own way, was Judd.
Both of these gentlemen were known to have been childless, and thus eligible for
uccession of Handmaids. Professor Wade and I have speculated in our joint paper, "Th
otion of 'Seed' in Early Gilead," that both – like many of the Commanders – had com
contact with a sterility-causing virus that was developed by secret pre-Gilead gen

f caviar used by top officials in Moscow. (The experiment was abandoned after th
pheres of Influence Accord, because the virus was considered too uncontrollable an
herefore too dangerous by many, although some wished to sprinkle it over India.)
However, neither Judd nor Waterford was married to a woman who was or ever ha
een known either as "Pam" or as "Serena Joy." This latter appears to have been
omewhat malicious invention by our author. Judd's wife's name was Bambi Mae, an
Waterford's was Thelma. The latter had, however, once worked as a televisio
ersonality of the type described. We know this from Limpkin, who makes several snid
marks about it. The regime itself took pains to cover up such former lapses fro
rthodoxy by the spouses of its elite.
The evidence on the whole favours Waterford. We know, for instance, that he met h
nd, probably soon after the events our author describes, in one of the earliest purge
e was accused of liberal tendencies, of being in possession of a substantial an
nauthorized collection of heretical pictorial and literary materials, and of harbouring

levising them, so the events were recorded in England via satellite and are o

ear enough to establish that his hair was indeed grey.
As for the subversive Waterford was accused of harbouring, this could have bee
Offred" herself, as her flight would have placed her in this category. More likely it wa
Nick," who, by the evidence of the very existence of the tapes, must have helpe
Offred" to escape. The way in which he was able to do this marks him as a member
he shadowy Mayday underground, which was not identical with the Undergroun
emaleroad but had connections with it. The latter was purely a rescue operation, th

--- PAGE 234 ---
rmer quasi-military. A number of Mayday operatives are known to have infiltrated th
ileadean power structure at the highest levels, and the placement of one of the
embers as chauffeur to Waterford would certainly have been a coup; a double coup, a
Nick" must have been at the same time a member of the Eyes, as such chauffeurs an
ersonal servants often were. Waterford would, of course, have been aware of this, bu
s all high-level Commanders were automatically directors of the Eyes, he would n
ave paid a great deal of attention to it and would not have let it interfere with h
fraction of what he considered to be minor rules. Like most early Gilead Commande
ho were later purged, he considered his position to be above attack. The style
iddle Gilead was more cautious.
This is our guesswork. Supposing it to be correct – supposing, that is, that Waterfor
as indeed the "Commander" – many gaps remain. Some of them could have been fille
y our anonymous author, had she had a different turn of mind. She could have told u
uch about the workings of the Gileadean empire, had she had the instincts of
porter or a spy. What would we not give, now, for even twenty pages or so
rintout from Waterford's private computer! However, we must be grateful for an
umbs the Goddess of History has designed to vouchsafe us.
As for the ultimate fate of our narrator, it remains obscure. Was she smuggled ov
he border of Gilead, into what was then Canada, and did she make her way thence t
ngland? This would have been wise, as the Canada of that time did not wish t
ntagonize its powerful neighbour, and there were roundups and extraditions of suc
fugees. If so, why did she not take her taped narrative with her? Perhaps her journe
as sudden; perhaps she feared interception. On the other hand, she may have bee
captured. If she did indeed reach England, why did she not make her story public, a
o many did upon reaching the outside world? She may have feared retaliation again
Luke," supposing him to have been still alive (which is an improbability), or eve
gainst her daughter; for the Gileadean regime was not above such measures, and use
hem to discourage adverse publicity in foreign countries. More than one incautiou
fugee was known to receive a hand, ear, or foot, vacuum-packed express, hidden in
r instance, a tin of coffee. Or perhaps she was among those escaped Handmaids wh
ad difficulty adjusting to life in the outside world, once they got there, after th
rotected existence they had led. She may have become, like them, a recluse. We do n
We can only deduce, also, the motivations for "Nick's" engineering of her escape. W
an assume that once her companion Ofglen's association with Mayday had bee
scovered, he himself was in some jeopardy, for as he well knew, as a member of th
yes, Offred herself was certain to be interrogated. The penalties for unauthorize
xual activity with a Handmaid were severe, nor would his status as an Eye necessari
rotect him. Gilead society was Byzantine in the extreme, and any transgression migh
e used against one by one's undeclared enemies within the regime. He could, of cours
ave assassinated her himself, which might have been the wiser course, but the huma
eart remains a factor, and, as we know, both of them thought she might be pregnan

--- PAGE 235 ---
y him. What male of the Gilead period could resist the possibility of fatherhood, s
dolent of status, so highly prized? Instead, he called in a rescue team of Eyes, wh
ay or may not have been authentic but in any case were under his orders. In doing s
e may well have brought about his own downfall. This too we shall never know.
Did our narrator reach the outside world safely and build a new life for herself? O
as she discovered in her attic hiding place, arrested, sent to the Colonies or t
ezebel's, or even executed? Our document, though in its own way eloquent, is on the
ubjects mute. We may call Eurydice forth from the world of the dead, but we cann
ake her answer; and when we turn to look at her we glimpse her only for a momen
efore she slips from our grasp and flees. As all historians know, the past is a gre
arkness, and filled with echoes. Voices may reach us from it; but what they say to us
mbued with the obscurity of the matrix out of which they come; and, try as we may, w
annot always decipher them precisely in the clearer light of our own day.

George Whrende

argaret Atwood was born in Ottawa in 1939, and grew up in northern Quebec ar
ontario, and later in Toronto. She has lived in numerous cities in Canada, the U.S., ar
irope.

She is the author of more than forty books - novels, short stories, poetry, litera:
iticism, social history, and books for children.

Atwood's work is acclaimed internationally and has been published around the worl
er novels include The Handmaid's Tale and Cat's Eye — both shortlisted for the Book
'ize; The Robber Bride; Alias Grace, winner of the prestigious Giller Prize in Canada ar
e Premio Mondello in Italy, and a finalist for the Booker Prize, the Orange Prize, ar
e International IMPAC Dublin Literary Award; The Blind Assassin, winner of tl
»oker Prize and a finalist for the International IMPAC Dublin Literary Award; ar
ryx and Crake, a finalist for The Giller Prize and the Man Booker Prize. Atwood is tl
cipient of numerous honours, such as the Sunday Times Award for Literary Excellen:

the U.K., the National Arts Club Medal of Honor for Literature in the U.S., |]
1evalier dans l'Ordre des Arts et des Lettres in France, and she was the first winner
e London Literary Prize. She has received honorary degrees from universities acro
anada, and one from Oxford University in England.

Margaret Atwood lives in Toronto with novelist Graeme Gibson.
